> [!definition] Flattening
> A data handling technique to transform multi-dimensional data into 1D.

We use this in neural networks so the input is a single vector, making weighted sum calculation easier. Usually we want to create a large matrix where every image or piece of data is one column.

> [!eg] Example
> If we have a dataset of shape `(209, 64, 64, 3)`, that means we have 209 images 64x64 resolution in RGB.
> We flatten this by using `.reshape()`
>
> ```python
> data_flat = data.reshape(data.shape[0], -1).T # data.shape[0] = 209
> ```
>
> This tells numpy I want to reshape the matrix to have 209 rows and figure out how to flatten the rest ($64 \times 64 \times 3$) into 1 dimension. The final shape being `(209, 12288)`. We can just use `-1` and let numpy figure it out itself, we would've got the same result if we used `.reshape(209, 12288)`.
>
> Transpose to make the rows into the columns.

> [!question] Why do we need to transpose and don't just reshape it into columns instantly?
> Python stores arrays using [[Row-Major]] order, so if we had asked numpy to do `data.reshape(-1, 209)` (an array of rows with length 209) it would start from the first image and write it's first 209 pixels to the first row then continue, so in the end our data is all scrambled and we don't have the structure we wanted of one image per column.
